People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

That's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, how has your mood been? Have you been feeling sad, down, or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Um... no. Not really. I mean, my mood is fine, I guess. I'm just... frustrated with the work stuff. But I wouldn't say I feel sad or empty. Just normal.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on tasks that require you to focus, like reading through audit documents or reviewing reports, do you find it hard to concentrate or do your thoughts wander easily?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I suppose so. If I'm trying to review an audit file, it doesn't take much. Like a Slack ping, or... someone talking outside my office. Even just, um, a thought about my marathon training. It just... pulls me completely off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting, tapping your fingers or feet, or having trouble sitting still, even when you're trying to focus on work?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I guess. When I'm in meetings, I usually tap my foot under the table... or click my pen. I guess I shift around in the chair a lot, too. My colleague actually joked recently that she can hear my leg going. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with concentration and fidgeting? Were you like this as a child or teenager, or did this start more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: Um... I suppose it's always been there. Even when I was a kid... elementary school, maybe. I always needed reminders to actually finish things. My mom still brings it up sometimes. She jokes that I could get a perfect score on a test but completely forget to turn in the project that was sitting right there in my backpack. So... yeah, it's not new.\n\nDoctor: When you were younger, did teachers or your parents ever describe you as hyperactive, overly energetic, or say that you were 'always on the go' or 'driven by a motor'?\n\nPatient: I guess so. So... yeah, I suppose 'always on the go' fits.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the work situation you mentioned, how much do these difficulties with focus, organization, and staying on task affect other areas of your life—like your relationships with your husband, your volunteer work, or managing things at home?\n\nPatient: Um... But then... I'll find out days later that the final email, or a review note, or a form just... never actually got done. So... yeah. That's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, how has your mood been? Have you been feeling sad, down, or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Um... no. Not really. I mean, my mood is fine, I guess. I'm just... frustrated with the work stuff. But I wouldn't say I feel sad or empty. Just normal.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on tasks that require you to focus, like reading through audit documents or reviewing reports, do you find it hard to concentrate or do your thoughts wander easily?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I suppose so. If I'm trying to review an audit file, it doesn't take much. Like a Slack ping, or... someone talking outside my office. Even just, um, a thought about my marathon training. It just... pulls me completely off track.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting, tapping your fingers or feet, or having trouble sitting still, even when you're trying to focus on work?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I guess. When I'm in meetings, I usually tap my foot under the table... or click my pen. I guess I shift around in the chair a lot, too. My colleague actually joked recently that she can hear my leg going. So... yes.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with concentration and fidgeting? Were you like this as a child or teenager, or did this start more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: Um... I suppose it's always been there. Even when I was a kid... elementary school, maybe. My report cards always said things like... I was smart but I lost assignments a lot. Or I'd rush and miss details. \n\nI guess I talked out of turn, too. And... I always needed reminders to actually finish things. My mom still brings it up sometimes. She jokes that I could get a perfect score on a test but completely forget to turn in the project that was sitting right there in my backpack. So... yeah, it's not new.\n\nDoctor: When you were younger, did teachers or your parents ever describe you as hyperactive, overly energetic, or say that you were 'always on the go' or 'driven by a motor'?\n\nPatient: I guess so. Um... yeah, probably. It's... it's still like that, honestly. Like on weekends... I'll tell my husband I'm just going to rest. But then I... I don't know, I just bounce around. I'll go from doing laundry to checking emails, then... go for a run, or work on volunteer planning. I don't really stop until I'm exhausted. So... yeah, I suppose 'always on the go' fits.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the work situation you mentioned, how much do these difficulties with focus, organization, and staying on task affect other areas of your life—like your relationships with your husband, your volunteer work, or managing things at home?\n\nPatient: Um... I suppose it affects things a fair amount. \n\nLike at work... I know you asked about other areas, but... the mistakes and the unfinished follow-ups, they actually affected a client review recently. Yeah.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to elementary school—say, around age 7 or 8—were you already having these kinds of problems with attention, organization, and finishing tasks, or did they develop later?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah. Like I mentioned earlier, it was already happening back then. Around seven or eight, I guess. \n\nThat's when those report cards started. The ones where teachers said I was bright but... kept losing my assignments, or rushing and missing small details. And talking out of turn. I always needed reminders. And... like I said about my mom joking about the backpack thing... acing the test but forgetting to turn in the actual project. \n\nSo... yes. It didn't just develop later. I just... kind of got through it.\n\nDoctor: By the time you were around 12 years old, were these problems with attention, finishing tasks, and fidgeting already causing you real trouble—like affecting your grades, getting you in trouble at school, or causing problems with friends?\n\nPatient: Um... I was just... going to school.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your school years—by age 12, were there times when your teachers or parents felt these attention or organization problems were actually getting in the way of your schoolwork, or causing enough trouble that they were concerned about it?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I mean... That's about it.\n\nDoctor: When you were around 12 years old, did these problems with attention and organization actually affect your grades or cause you to fall behind in school, or did they keep you from doing things you wanted to do?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. She jokes that I could get a perfect score on a test but completely forget to turn in the project that was sitting right there in my backpack. So... yeah, it's not new.\n\nDoctor: When you were younger, did teachers or your parents ever describe you as hyperactive, overly energetic, or say that you were 'always on the go' or 'driven by a motor'?\n\nPatient: I guess so. Um... yeah, probably. It's... it's still like that, honestly. Like on weekends... I'll tell my husband I'm just going to rest. But then I... I don't know, I just bounce around. I'll go from doing laundry to checking emails, then... go for a run, or work on volunteer planning. I don't really stop until I'm exhausted. So... yeah, I suppose 'always on the go' fits.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the work situation you mentioned, how much do these difficulties with focus, organization, and staying on task affect other areas of your life—like your relationships with your husband, your volunteer work, or managing things at home?\n\nPatient: Um... I suppose it affects things a fair amount. \n\nLike at work... I know you asked about other areas, but... the mistakes and the unfinished follow-ups, they actually affected a client review recently. I'm... well, I'm worried people are questioning if I can even handle the promotion I'm up for. It's... not great. \n\nAnd at home... um... my husband just handles the bills now. And other commitments I said I'd take care of. He had to take them over because... well, he can't really count on me to finish them reliably. So... he just does it. Yeah.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to elementary school—say, around age 7 or 8—were you already having these kinds of problems with attention, organization, and finishing tasks, or did they develop later?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah. Like I mentioned earlier, it was already happening back then. Around seven or eight, I guess. \n\nThat's when those report cards started. It was there in elementary school.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the work mistakes affected a client review and your husband had to take over bill payments. Looking back to when you were in school—did these attention and organization problems cause similar kinds of trouble for you academically or socially back then?\n\nPatient: Um... I don't really know. I mean, I guess I got in trouble for talking sometimes, or... forgetting things. But I still got decent grades eventually, so... I don't think it was a huge issue. I just... kind of got through it.\n\nDoctor: By the time you were around 12 years old, were these problems with attention, finishing tasks, and fidgeting already causing you real trouble—like affecting your grades, getting you in trouble at school, or causing problems with friends?\n\nPatient: Um... I don't know if it was actually causing problems with friends or... anything like that by the time I was twelve. I just... I don't recall it being a major issue. I was just... going to school.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your school years—by age 12, were there times when your teachers or parents felt these attention or organization problems were actually getting in the way of your schoolwork, or causing enough trouble that they were concerned about it?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I mean... I think I already said this. I kept up fine.\n\nDoctor: When you were around 12 years old, did you ever get in trouble at school—like detention, being sent to the principal's office, or losing privileges—because of the attention problems, rushing, or talking out of turn?\n\nPatient: Um... no. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
